© Andrey_Popov / Shutterstock.com
Alphabet (NASDAQ:GOOG | GOOG Price Prediction) has become the billionaire trade of 2026. Recent 13F cycles show Warren Buffett was far from the only billionaire buying Alphabet stock in Q2, joining Bill Ackman and Stanley Druckenmiller rotating into the search giant.
Our 24/7 Wall St. price target for Alphabet is $437.39, implying 27.99% upside from the current $341.75 share price. The model rates GOOG a buy with 90% confidence.
GOOG gained 70.83% over the trailing year against 9.05% year-to-date, though shares consolidated recently, off 0.52% over the past week.
The Q2 FY2026 report delivered $119.80 billion in revenue, up 24.23% year over year, with EPS of $9.11 against a $3.04 estimate. Google Cloud accelerated to 82% growth with $24.8 billion in revenue and a 35.6% operating margin, while cloud backlog hit $514 billion. This backdrop explains why multiple billionaires are piling in.
Bulls see a $454.48 upside scenario, roughly 33% above spot. Google Cloud is compounding, with nearly 90% of the Fortune 100 using Gemini Enterprise. The Gemini App has 950 million monthly active users, and API traffic scaled to 22 billion tokens per minute, up from 16 billion just a quarter earlier.
TPU system sales, Waymo’s 500,000+ weekly rides, and 350 million paid subscriptions add optionality. On the sell side, 13 strong buys, 44 buys, 6 holds, and zero sells form one of the cleanest analyst books in mega-cap tech.
The bear scenario points to $361.62, essentially flat to today. The chief concern is capex intensity. Q2 free cash flow ran to negative $5.855 billion, with capex up 100.14% year over year to $44.92 billion. Long-term debt climbed from roughly $16 billion a year ago to about $100 billion, buybacks were suspended, and CFO Anat Ashkenazi warned third-party capacity will “put some pressure on operating margins for cloud.”
Bulls counter that this spending funds a supply-constrained AI backlog, so the free-cash-flow drop reads as strategic reinvestment (and it is worth remembering the picks-and-shovels names on the other side of that spending, which we broke down in a free report on seven AI infrastructure suppliers that aren’t chipmakers).
Alphabet screens cheap against Magnificent Seven peers on trailing earnings. Microsoft (NASDAQ:MSFT) fetches a trailing P/E of roughly 27, backed by Azure crossing $100 billion in annual revenue and a $678 billion commercial RPO backlog. That backlog rivals Alphabet’s $514 billion, yet MSFT trades at nearly double the multiple.
Meta Platforms (NASDAQ:META) sits at P/E near 20, but Q2 EPS missed expectations on a cost surge, and Reality Labs remains a multi-billion drag. Against this set, GOOG’s P/E of 14 makes our 24/7 Wall St. price target look conservative.
Our 24/7 Wall St. price target of $437.39 offers GOOG one of the cleanest risk/reward setups in mega-cap tech, well above the bear scenario. The constructive path requires free cash flow stabilizing in H2 2026 and cloud margins holding above 30%.
The risk case builds if capex creeps beyond $175 to $185 billion 2026 guidance without a clear TPU revenue ramp in 2027. The buy call carries 90% confidence.
Here is where our model projects GOOG could trade, assuming current growth trajectories and market conditions hold.
These projections assume Alphabet executes on its AI-first strategy. Significant upside or downside could result from Waymo commercialization, antitrust remedies in Search, or a sharper-than-expected step-down in AI capex intensity.

							CBRS crashed 40% from its May high after Q2 missed estimates, yet 10 of 11 analysts still rate it Buy with a consensus target implying 57% upside.													

							NVDA fell just 7% and AMD dropped 10% over the same stretch, confirming CBRS's brutal selloff was company-specific, not a sector-wide retreat.													

							A $25 billion backlog anchored by a $20 billion OpenAI deal and plans to triple 2027 revenue form the backbone of the bull case.													

Act now: the analyst who called NVIDIA in 2010 just named his top 10 AI stocks — and Cerebras Systems Inc. Class A Common Stock didn't make the cut. Grab the names FREE today.


					Sending You to Google News in 3

© 24/7 Wall St.
Cerebras Systems (NASDAQ:CBRS) currently trades near $185.43, while the average Wall Street price target sits at $291.64. That gap implies roughly 57% of upside to consensus.
Cerebras builds wafer-scale AI systems that sidestep the memory and packaging bottlenecks throttling standard GPU clusters. Its wafer-scale engine architecture avoids HBM memory, CoWoS packaging, and 3nm fabrication, and its customer roster runs through OpenAI, AWS, AMD, and CrowdStrike.
Wall Street expected explosive inference demand post-IPO. Instead, the stock has become one of the choppiest names in AI infrastructure, and that dislocation gives the price-to-target gap real teeth.
Shares fell 26.41% in a single week after Q2 2026 results missed expectations. Revenue of $180.11 million fell short of the analyst estimate of $193.55 million, and GAAP EPS of -2.98 came in far below the consensus loss of -0.1801.
The bigger issue was margin guidance. Management guided Q3 core operating margin to negative 25% to negative 23%, down from negative 16% in Q2. On a widely read Reddit thread, traders summed up the reaction as “Cerebras falls 10% after chipmaker forecasts shrinking margin in first earnings report since IPO.”
Shares are now down 40.39% from a May high of $311.07. The move was company-specific. Semiconductors broadly held up over the same window.
Even with shares tumbling, analysts have barely blinked. UBS analyst Timothy Arcuri holds a $330 Street-high 12-month target, implying roughly 78% upside from current levels. His thesis rests on three pillars: the pending launch of Cerebras’s CS-4 wafer-scale platform with orders-of-magnitude higher SRAM memory bandwidth than GPU clusters, hyper-growth revenue anchored by enterprise and hyperscale backlog, and gross margin scaling into the 40%+ range as yields mature.
The backlog case is real. Remaining performance obligations sit at $25.4 billion, anchored by a multi-year OpenAI agreement for 750 megawatts of inference compute valued at more than $20 billion. Management raised full-year 2026 core revenue guidance to $880 to $890 million and plans to more than triple revenue in 2027.
CEO Andrew Feldman stated: “Fast tokens are in demand and command a premium at market.” He also flagged a disaggregated inference solution with AMD (NASDAQ:AMD | AMD Price Prediction) Helios deploying in Q4 and an AWS Bedrock rollout scheduled for Q1 2027, both viewed as catalysts for second-half re-rating.
Of the 11 analysts covering CBRS, 3 rate it Strong Buy, 7 Buy, and 1 Hold, with no Sells. Recent revisions have been reiterations rather than cuts.
Cerebras fell alone. NVIDIA (NASDAQ:NVDA) and AMD, its two closest listed AI-compute peers, pulled back over the same stretch but nowhere near as violently.
NVIDIA sits at $208.48, down 7.35% on the week. Its consensus target of $304.73 implies about 46% upside, with analyst posture skewed heavily to Buys and revisions trending up on Blackwell demand.
AMD trades at $456.75 after slipping 9.73% last week. Its consensus target of $613.09 implies roughly 34% upside, backed by a Buy-heavy rating mix and recent target raises tied to the Instinct MI450 ramp.
The largest analyst-implied upside across the group sits with Cerebras. That says the Street thinks the selloff was overdone. It also says Cerebras carries the highest execution risk in the trio.
Cerebras currently trades at $185.43 against a consensus target of $291.64, with 11 covering analysts and 91% bullish sentiment. Analyst targets are not guarantees, but the skew here is unusually one-sided.
Recent performance shows the pain. CBRS is down 26.41% over the past week and 40.39% from its May peak. The S&P 500 is up 11.96% year to date and down just 1.19% on the week. That is idiosyncratic weakness on a scale the broader market simply is not showing.
Analyst ratings breakdown:
The bull case rests on the CS-4 launch landing cleanly, the AWS Bedrock and AMD Helios integrations shipping on schedule, and management executing on tripling revenue in 2027. Q3 is guided as the low point for gross margins. If Q4 inflects as expected, the second-half re-rating alone gets the stock most of the way to consensus.
The bear case centers on the $377 million in stock-based compensation and concentration around OpenAI make the earnings picture uninvestable at this stage. If capacity ramp slips or a major customer renegotiates, the $291 target compresses fast.
I lean cautiously constructive. The $25.4 billion RPO figure and the AWS-AMD-OpenAI trifecta are too large to dismiss, and the risk/reward at $185 tilts toward the upside case. Size small and stomach volatility. This is not a table-pounder. 95,2026-08-25,"OpenAI CEO Sam Altman says that AI is still awaiting its iPhone moment, and that he needs to learn a key lesson from Apple.
He also admits that he was overly ambitious in his idea of how quickly AI would disrupt the economy, but thinks that, paradoxically, the tech will create new opportunities for businesses that don’t use it … 
Altman made the comments in a David Senra podcast episode reported by TNW. He likened the current AI era to the smartphone days prior to the launch of the iPhone. Many of us in the tech world owned Windows CE, Nokia, and PalmOS devices from the mid-1990s, but it was the iPhone that transformed smartphones from nerdy pocket computers to mass-market devices.
Altman compared the current phase to smartphones before Apple arrived. He had a Palm Treo around 2003 […] “We have all of the technological pieces, but we have not had the iPhone moment of completely changing how someone interfaces with technology.”
He says that AI companies, including his own, haven’t yet found the magic formula to really bring the tech into everyday consumer life.
Steve Jobs famously said that one of the key reasons Apple was successful was that the company didn’t try to do too much, saying “no” to a thousand good ideas in order to fully focus on the very few products where the company said “yes.”
Altman referenced this, saying he was bad at killing good ideas in order to protect the great ones.
He believes one of the key changes we are going to see is “the greatest boom in people starting smaller businesses that we have ever seen.”
In part, this will be because of the things AI makes possible, but he also predicts a less obvious outcome. He says some businesses will succeed “precisely because they have nothing to do with AI; people will want authentic, non-technological experiences.”
The episode sounds well worth listening to, and I have it in my queue.
